--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
I'll analyze the results and design 14 machines for round 2.

**Reasoning:**

The champion is a pure Stop (B=1) machine with score 13985, which means it's always stopped - accumulating phase 1 penalty (~500 steps × 28 robots = ~14000) but zero phase 2 penalty. This makes sense as a baseline.

The key insight: we need robots to MOVE during phase 1 and STOP when they perceive GREEN (color 2) in phase 2. The best structure is:
- State 0: Moving (Exploration/Repulsion) 
- State 1: Stop, triggered by perceiving green (C=7, l=2)

The transition from moving→stop should fire on perceiving green with high probability (p close to 1.0). We also need a backup transition from stop→moving in case it triggers early (though green only appears at switch time).

A 2-state FSM: Explore → (on green perception) → Stop should score much better than 13985 if it works correctly. Phase 1 cost ≈ 0 (moving), phase 2 cost ≈ few steps to detect green × 28 robots.

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
**Reasoning:**

The champion (score 9062) uses GoToColor(magenta/5) in state 0, transitioning to Stop on perceiving green (color 2) with p=1.0, plus a tiny random fallback. The key insight is that GoToColor keeps robots moving well during phase 1, and the green-perception trigger reliably switches them to Stop in phase 2.

The remaining score (~9062) likely comes from: ~500 steps × 28 robots where some robots are momentarily stationary (GoToColor can pause when no target), plus the latency between green appearing and all robots perceiving it.

Strategy:
1. Keep the champion exactly
2. Exploit: vary clr (color being chased), rwm, vel, and the fallback probability
3. Try GoToColor with different target colors to keep robots maximally mobile
4. Try 3-state machines: move → (on green) → stop, with a secondary moving state
5. The stop→move back-transition probability should be near 0 (tiny, to avoid re-entering moving after stopping)

The transition `n1x0` destination error from before: in a 2-state FSM, leaving state 1, the only other state is 0, so destination index D=0 means state 0. This should be correct. I'll be careful with all parameters.

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
**Reasoning:**

The champion (8557) uses GoToColor(magenta/5) → Stop on green perception, with p0x1=0.001 (tiny random fallback) and p1x0=0.0005 (very tiny stop→move). The second best (9050) uses GoAwayColor(green/2) which also keeps robots moving well.

Key observations:
- GoToColor with non-green color works well (keeps robots moving toward colored walls/robots)
- The green-perception trigger (C=7, l=2, p=1.0) is the critical mechanism
- Very small stop→move probability (0.0005) is better than 0.001
- The fallback random transition from moving state (p≈0.001) seems to help slightly

Exploitation: vary the fallback probability (try 0.0005, 0.003), try p1x0=0.0001 (even smaller), try different GoToColor targets (1,3,4,6), try GoAwayColor variations.

Exploration: try 3-state machines where state 0 = GoToColor(magenta), state 1 = Stop, state 2 = Exploration as backup mover; try adding a neighbour-count condition as secondary trigger.

The champion (8331) uses GoToColor(magenta/5) with green-perception trigger (p=1.0) plus tiny random fallback (p0x1=0.001) and very tiny stop→move (p1x0=0.0001). The trend is clear: smaller p1x0 is better (reduces accidental re-activation after stopping). The fallback p0x1=0.001 seems optimal.

- GoToColor(magenta/5) beats Exploration for phase 1 movement
- Green perception (C=7, l=2) is the right trigger

- p0x1 (fallback) around 0.001-0.003 range

Exploitation ideas:
- Try p1x0=0.00001 or even 0 (no back-transition at all - but need at least 1 transition per state, so use p=0.0001 or less)
- Try p0x1=0.002 with p1x0=0.0001
- Try adding a second green-perception transition with different probability
- Try GoToColor with color 6 (cyan) or 3 (blue)

Exploration:
- Try a machine where stop state has NO back-transition issue by using a very stable stop
- Try GoAwayColor(green) as moving state since it actively avoids green walls

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
**Reasoning:**

The champion (7355) uses GoToColor(magenta/5) with p0x1=0.0015 (random fallback) and p1x0=0.0001 (tiny back-transition). The second best (7426) uses p0x1=0.002. Both have p1x0=0.0001.

Key findings:
- The random fallback p0x1 around 0.0015-0.002 is optimal (helps robots that get stuck near walls keep moving)
- p1x0=0.0001 is better than 0.0005 (less accidental re-activation)
- Having NO fallback (p0x1 only via green) is bad (score 11656) - robots get stuck
- GoAwayColor(green/2) also works well (7756)
- The second green-perception transition (redundant C=7, l=2) was bad

Exploitation:
- Try p0x1 values around 0.0012, 0.0015, 0.0018, 0.002, 0.0025
- Try p1x0=0.00005 (even smaller)
- Try different clr values with p0x1≈0.0015

Exploration:
- Try a 3-state machine: GoToColor(magenta) → Stop → Exploration (tiny prob), with green trigger on both moving states
- Try combining the random fallback with a neighbour condition

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
**Reasoning:**

The champion (7355) is stable at p0x1=0.0015, p1x0=0.0001. The best performers this round cluster around p0x1=0.0015-0.002. The score variance between runs is high (same FSM scores 7751 vs 7355 in different rounds), suggesting stochasticity plays a big role.

Key insights:
- p0x1=0.0015-0.002 seems optimal (0.003 is worse, too many random stops)
- p1x0=0.0001 is the sweet spot (smaller = fewer accidental re-activations)
- GoToColor(magenta/5) and GoToColor(cyan/6) and GoAwayColor(green/2) all work well
- 3-state machines hurt when the stop state self-loops (worst performers)
- The structure is converging - need to fine-tune around the champion

Strategy: Focus exploitation tightly around p0x1=0.0013-0.002, p1x0=0.00005-0.0001. Try a few color variants. Try p1x0=0 effectively by using an extremely small value. Explore a 2-transition stop state that reinforces stopping on green perception.

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
**Reasoning:**

The champion (7295) is very stable now. The top performers are all very similar 2-state machines with GoToColor→Stop on green perception. Key observations:

- GoToColor(magenta/5, clr=5) and GoToColor(cyan/6, clr=6) both score 7295 identically
- GoAwayColor(green/2) scores 7329 - slightly worse but competitive
- p0x1=0.0015 and p0x1=0.002 both work well
- p1x0=0.0001 is optimal
- LED color cle0=5 (magenta) is WORSE (9709) - the LED color matters for robot-robot interaction!
- cle0=4 (yellow) is the best LED color

The scores are converging tightly. The remaining gap from optimal is likely due to:
1. Latency in perceiving green (robots facing away from green wall)
2. The tiny p0x1 causing some robots to stop randomly during phase 1

Exploitation: Fine-tune p0x1 around 0.0013-0.0018, try p1x0=0.00005, try different clr values with cle0=4. Try combining green-perception with a second condition in stop state.

Exploration: Try a variant where the moving state also uses GoToColor targeting non-green colors that robots can see (like walls), ensuring maximum movement. Try p0x1 with a slightly different value.

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
**Reasoning:**

The champion (7295) has been stable for 2 rounds. The scores are tightly clustered around 7295-7617. Key observations:

- The optimal structure is firmly: GoToColor(cle0=4) → Stop, with p0x1≈0.0015-0.002, p1x0=0.0001
- clr0=5 (magenta) and clr0=6 (cyan) tie at 7295/7576
- clr0=4 (yellow) is bad (9545) - probably because robots emit yellow LEDs so GoToColor(yellow) makes them chase each other
- GoAwayColor(green/2) is consistently good (~7557)
- Double random fallback (3 transitions) is worse (8094)
- p0x1 sweet spot seems to be 0.0015-0.0017

With only 2 rounds left, I should focus tightly on exploitation around the champion while trying a few targeted variations:
- Fine-tune p0x1 between 0.0014-0.0016 
- Try p1x0=0.00008 (between 0.00005 and 0.0001)
- Try clr0=3 (blue) and clr0=1 (red) which haven't been thoroughly tested
- Try GoAwayColor(green) with p0x1=0.0017

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
**Reasoning:**

This is the final round. The champion (7295) has been stable. The scores vary due to stochasticity - the same FSM scores differently each round (7295 vs 7660 for the same machine). 

Key findings across all rounds:
- GoToColor(magenta/5, cle0=4) with p0x1=0.0015, p1x0=0.0001 is the champion
- GoAwayColor(green/2, cle0=4) with p0x1=0.0015 scores very similarly (7325)
- GoToColor(cyan/6, cle0=4) also ties
- p0x1=0.0015-0.002 is the sweet spot
- p1x0=0.0001 is optimal
- The variation between runs is ~300-400 points for identical machines

For the final round, I want to maximize coverage of the best parameter region and give the champion multiple chances to re-score. I'll submit many close variants of the top configurations, all with p0x1 in [0.0014, 0.002] and p1x0=0.0001, using the best-performing behaviors (B=8 clr=5, B=8 clr=6, B=9 clr=2).

################################################################################
# PROMPT SYSTEME
################################################################################

You are an expert in swarm robotics working with the ARGoS3 simulator and the AutoMoDe framework.
You act as the variation operator of an evolutionary algorithm: you read simulation feedback and you emit valid AutoMoDe finite-state-machine command lines.

# 1. THE MISSION
OBJECTIVE. This mission has TWO SUCCESSIVE PHASES separated by a signal, and the scoring rule is REVERSED between them. The score is MINIMISED.

PHASE 1 - BEFORE THE SIGNAL. At the start of each run the loop function draws a secret switch time uniformly between 400 and 600 simulation steps. Until that step, the score gains +1 for every robot that is STATIONARY. During phase 1 the robots must KEEP MOVING.

PHASE 2 - AFTER THE SIGNAL. Exactly at the switch time, one randomly chosen wall block of the arena turns GREEN. From that step on the rule flips: the score gains +1 for every robot that is MOVING. The robots must STOP and stay still for the rest of the run.

A robot counts as moving if it travelled more than 0.0005 m since the previous step.

CONSEQUENCE FOR THE FSM. Stopping immediately is a BAD strategy: it costs about one point per robot for each of the ~500 steps of phase 1. Moving for the whole run is equally bad, for the ~700 steps of phase 2. The machine needs at least one moving state and one stopped state, and the transition into the stopped state must be driven by PERCEIVING THE GREEN BLOCK. A transition based only on a fixed probability cannot be accurate, because the switch time is redrawn at every run. The floor is uniformly GRAY, so floor conditions carry no information in this mission.

# 2. THE ARENA FOR THIS RUN
The arena is a regular polygon with 8 walls, inner radius 0.88 m. 28 e-puck robots start scattered inside a disc of radius 0.71 m around the centre, with random orientations. Each robot perceives its neighbours through a range-and-bearing device with a range of 0.58 m: neighbour-count conditions only see robots closer than that. A run lasts 1200 simulation steps (120 s at 10 steps per second).
For reference, the published setup uses 20 robots, an inner radius of 0.91 m and a range of 0.50 m, so thresholds tuned for that setup may need adjusting here.

# 3. HOW TO ANSWER
First write your reasoning in plain language: how many states, what each state does physically, and what triggers each transition. Two or three sentences per machine is enough.
Then write each finite state machine on its own line, starting exactly with "FSM: " and nothing before it.

# 4. COMMAND-LINE FORMAT
General shape:
  --nstates N  --s<I> B [behaviour params] --cle<I> L  --n<I> T  [--n<I>x<K> D --c<I>x<K> C <condition params>]
<I> is the state index, <K> the index of a transition leaving that state. Both start at 0.

## A. Global
* `--nstates N` : number of states, integer in [1..4].

## B. Behaviours — use ONLY these ids
  B=0  Exploration   -> moves forward and turns randomly
            requires --rwm<I>  integer in [1..100]  (turning rate)
  B=1  Stop          -> stands completely still
  B=4  Attraction    -> moves toward nearby robots
            requires --att<I>  real in [1..5]  (attraction gain)
  B=5  Repulsion     -> moves away from nearby robots
            requires --rep<I>  real in [1..5]  (repulsion gain)
  B=8  GoToColor     -> moves toward the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)
  B=9  GoAwayColor   -> moves away from the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)

Every state also takes an LED colour:
* `--cle<I> L` : one of {0,4,5,6}  (0=off, 1=red, 2=green, 3=blue, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan)

## C. Transitions
* `--n<I> T` : number of transitions leaving state <I>, integer in [1..4].
  Up to 4 transitions may leave the same state, each with a different condition type. A state that reacts to several different situations is often stronger than a state with one transition, and random sampling produces that variety by default.
* `--n<I>x<K> D` : destination, indexed among the OTHER states only.
  With 3 states, leaving state 1 the others are [0, 2]: D=0 targets state 0, D=1 targets state 2.
  With 2 states, leaving state 0 the only other state is state 1, so D must be 0.
* `--c<I>x<K> C` : condition type — use ONLY these ids
  C=0  floor under the robot is BLACK
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=1  floor under the robot is GRAY
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=2  floor under the robot is WHITE
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=3  number of neighbours ABOVE a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=4  number of neighbours BELOW a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=5  fires with a fixed probability at every step
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=7  the robot PERCEIVES a given colour with its camera
            requires --l<I>x<K>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (colour to look for)
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])

# 5. HARD RULES
1. Integers stay integers; real-valued parameters always carry a decimal point (1.0, not 1).
2. Every state that exists must declare `--n<I>`, and every declared transition must carry its destination, its condition type and that condition's parameters. A missing parameter crashes the simulator.
3. `--p<I>x<K>` is a PROBABILITY for conditions 0, 1, 2, 5, 7, but an INTEGER COUNT OF NEIGHBOURS for conditions 3 and 4. Never write 0.8 where a neighbour count is expected.
4. Never point a transition at its own state.

# 6. YOU ARE THE EVOLUTIONARY OPERATOR
This is a multi-round conversation on one real ARGoS3 optimisation run. The numerical optimiser is restarted from scratch at the beginning of every round, so exploration and exploitation are entirely up to you.

1. SURVIVAL (elitism): copy the current best-ever machine verbatim in your answer so its lineage is not lost.
2. EXPLOITATION: spend about half of your machines on small edits of the best performers — shift a probability, a threshold or a gain by roughly ten percent, and keep the topology.
3. EXPLORATION: spend the rest on genuinely different topologies or module combinations, to escape local optima.
4. RICHNESS: do not let every state settle on a single transition of the same condition type. Include candidates with two to four transitions per state mixing different condition types.
